Elavate is a multi-collagen supplement brand for women 40+, built around the changes that come with perimenopause and menopause. Our founder, Alex Philip, started it after getting fed up buying overpriced, low-quality, bad-tasting collagen for his mum.

Since launching in 2024 we've become one of the fastest-growing DTC brands in the UK, with over 35,000 five-star reviews. We launched in the US in 2026, and it's now our biggest focus for growth.

Our growth runs on video ads: UGC, influencer and celebrity talent content, made with an in-house team of creative strategists and video editors. The better the creators and the content, the faster we grow. That's where you come in.

Our creative strategists write the scripts and our editors turn footage into ads. You're the person in the middle who makes the content happen: finding the right creators, briefing and storyboarding every script, running influencer and high-production shoots with our agency partners, and making sure every video that comes back is right.

UGC: creators film themselves at home from our briefs, so the brief has to do all the directing

Influencer and high-production shoots: the two go hand in hand. We film well-known influencers and TV personalities with a professional crew, at a location picked for the look, with lighting, a teleprompter and make-up. These shoots make our long-form direct response ads, plus the hooks, product shots and B-roll that go with them

You'll work across both markets, the UK and the US, alongside our founder and creative team.

This is a communications, creators and systems role. You want to be in it every day: talking to creators, getting content made and getting deals done. Strategy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Find and sign the best creators in the UK and US

Source new UGC creators every week, mostly women 40+ who come across as natural, expressive and believable on camera

Vet properly before anyone gets a brief: watch their content, judge their delivery, setting and camera skills, and only put forward people you'd bet on

Get in touch fast and keep every conversation moving. The best creators get booked up quickly, so speed wins the deal

Negotiate good deals: strong rates, the right usage and whitelisting terms, and every deliverable written in (videos, hooks, raw files, B-roll)

Keep the pipeline full so our strategists never wait on talent

Build a database of the best UGC creators in the UK and US, and keep it up to date every week

Log every creator after every job: contact details, rates, niche, past jobs, how their content performed, delivery quality and whether we'd rebook them

Keep a bench of proven creators we can rebook at short notice

Keep an eye on creative performance in Motion, so you know which creators and videos are winning and can find, rebook and brief more like them

Turn finished scripts from our strategists into briefs a creator can film without guessing: a scene-by-scene storyboard, B-roll, visual direction, setting, outfits and props

Lock every brief before it goes out: one final version, a clear deadline, a reference video and links you've tested

Run a quick test clip before each full batch, so problems get caught before they cost us a refilm

Coordinate our influencer and high-production shoots

Work hand in hand with our content agency and shoot production agency: they run the day-to-day logistics on our crewed shoots, you make sure nothing gets missed

Run the threads with agents and talent managers on scheduling, call times and riders

Own the details: locations, props, product on set and a checklist to the crew before every shoot

Check every delivery against the script within 24 hours of filming: every hook, every section, resolution, lighting, framing and product shots

Post a written verdict for the team on what's good and exactly what's wrong (a thumbs-up isn't a check), so refilm calls get made fast

Once content is approved, get sign-off from the talent side, secure whitelisting access and hand clean files to our editors

This is what makes or breaks the role, so we're being specific.

You're very responsive and active on Slack. This is one of the most important parts of the role. When you're online, messages get a reply in minutes, not hours, even if it's just "on it, back to you by 4pm". Slow replies are how things slip and shoots go wrong.

You own it end to end. Some of our shoots cost thousands, so you treat every one like it's your own money. Nobody checks behind you: if something's about to fall through the cracks, you catch it first.

You confirm, you don't assume. A CC isn't a confirmation. When a time, location or script changes, you confirm it directly with every person involved.

You take in the full context. You listen to the whole voice note, watch the whole Loom and read the whole thread before you act. Then you ask the questions that matter.

You close loops. Sign-offs, follow-ups and tracker updates don't sit with you. If something's blocked, you flag it before anyone has to chase you.

You turn up. Every call, on time and prepared.

You ship, then improve. A solid brief out today beats a beautiful one next week.

You build systems. If you're doing the same manual task twice, you build a workflow for it (Notion, templates, AI) so the third time is faster.

You're there when it's live. On shoot days and urgent threads, you jump online outside your normal hours if something needs doing.

You take feedback well. Direct feedback, no ego, acted on the same day.

2+ years in UGC, creator or influencer coordination, at a DTC brand, a UGC or performance creative agency, or an influencer or talent agency

#7168. Students with discharged IDR loans now face huge tax bills

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Borrowers will be taxed on student debt canceled under the program in 2026 and could owe up to $12,000 more in April, per a new analysis.

Under the pandemic-era American Rescue Plan Act, discharged student loan debt did not count as income on federal taxes from Dec. 31, 2020, to Dec. 31, 2025. But since Congress allowed that provision to expire, IDR loans discharged in 2026 and beyond will appear on borrowers’ tax bills.

Protect Borrowers called on federal lawmakers to intervene and permanently exempt discharged IDR loans, as well as discharged loans through the Repayment Assistance Plan, from federal taxes in the next budget reconciliation process.

The average discharged IDR loan is worth about $50,000, according to the nonproft. Its analysis said that sum often outstrips the value of the initial loan, due to borrowers’ low income-based monthly payments not covering the cost of ballooning interest.

Single borrowers without children with discharged IDR loans would pay over $10,000 in federal taxes if they earned $40,000 in 2026 and nearly $20,000 if they earned $80,000.

After payroll and state taxes, these borrowers “could effectively see their take-home income cut nearly in half,” the report said.

Families with children and a lower household income could see the highest share of their annual earnings absorbed by the tax increase. A household of four with $40,000 in annual income can expect to pay an additional $10,558 on top of their base expected tax bill — $1,704 in new taxes and $8,854 in lost tax credits.

“Treating such cancelled debt as taxable income will hit many borrowers with an unprecedented tax bomb and force them to replace their debt to the U.S. Department of Education with debt to the Internal Revenue Service,” the analysis said.

The change is also expected to have a disproportionate impact on borrowers in the South, as they typically have larger loan balances and lower incomes than the U.S. average.

The massive expected increase in taxes will put most borrowers in an impossible financial position, after they’ve already “endured significant financial hardship for a majority of their adult lives,” Protect Borrowers said.

As of 2022, half of borrowers who had their IDR student debt canceled didn’t have a bank account or nonretirement savings, the nonprofit said, citing survey data from the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau.

Like Protect Borrowers, a coalition of Democratic Senators last November called on the Trump administration to exempt canceled IDR loans from income taxation.

Following Tuesday’s midterms, Democrats are set to gain control of both chambers of Congress and such a change could stand a better chance of passing. President Donald Trump still holds veto power.

#7165. Iowa public universities exceed revenue projections but brace for enrollment drop

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Despite tuition increases in the most recent fiscal year, leaders see challenges ahead amid demographic shifts, higher costs and lower state tax revenue.

The report noted that “strong enrollment growth drove increases in tuition and fee revenue” at Iowa’s three state universities during fiscal 2026, which ended June 30. Those revenues came in half a percentage point over the regents’ budget.

As for enrollment, head count across the three institutions came to 71,872 in fall 2025, an increase of about 2% from the previous year.

This fall, undergraduate enrollment at Iowa’s public universities increased by 0.2%, or 98 students, according to a separate report also prepared for the regents’ Nov. 11 meeting. But overall enrollment fell 0.5%, or by 352 students, driven by a loss of graduate enrollment.

Like the system overall, the University of Iowa and Iowa State University largely saw their undergraduate populations hold steady. But those numbers mask losses in new first-year undergraduate enrollment this fall — down by 5.3% at UI and 4.7% at Iowa State. Meanwhile, new first-year undergrads increased by 10.3% at the smaller University of Northern Iowa.

In the fiscal report, the regents staff invoked the long anticipated enrollment cliff, with the numbers of traditional-aged college students estimated to begin declining after this year.

While Iowa is expected to fare better than many of its neighboring states when it comes to demographics, it won’t be left unaffected.

The number of high school graduates in Iowa could decline 9% between 2026 and 2041, according to the Western Interstate Commission for Higher Education’s latest estimates, cited in the regents’ enrollment report. That compares to a projected 29% decline in Illinois, 13% decline in Minnesota and 16% decline in Wisconsin.

But state colleges don’t recruit in a vacuum. Regents staff pointed out in their report that other states “from which the Regent universities receive many out-of-state students are expected to experience much larger drops in their high school graduate population.”

That could weigh on Iowa universities’ finances as colleges compete for a shrinking pool of students in the region — and declines in out-of-state students would have outsized impacts on Iowa universities’ budgets given the higher tuition prices those students pay.

“While enrollment and retention rates remain strong, institutions are closely monitoring key indicators — including applications, campus visits and housing deposits — to inform long-term enrollment management planning,” the fiscal report said.

#7160. How to navigate AI adoption — with faculty and student input

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Artificial intelligence is transforming the mission of public education and “can’t just be implemented one way from the top,” one instructor said.

Colleges and universities nationwide have faced waves of student protests pushing back on institutional policies that embrace the use of artificial intelligence tools on campus.

That includes students and faculty at Columbia University who in May railed against the use of AI to announce graduates' names during commencement ceremonies, and students and employees at Emory University who in April called on administrators to remove AI-powered surveillance cameras from campus.

Universities have used AI tools to assist with a host of administrative functions, such as scheduling courses and nudging students to register for classes. In addition, many college officials are pushing to embed instruction on AI into curricula as businesses increasingly use generative AI platforms like OpenAI's ChatGPT and Google’s Gemini.

The embrace of AI has caused tension among students and faculty wary about its environmental, ethical, pedagogical, social and academic integrity impacts, said Victor Lee, faculty lead at the Stanford Accelerator for Learning's initiative to support educators in navigating the AI era.

Holding intentional conversations with students and faculty about AI use on campus can help ease concerns, said Lee.

Faculty members aren’t necessarily opposed to the technology, experts say, but training on the tools is spotty and they would like to be part of conversations about how their institutions use AI.

Robert Ovetz, a San José State University professor, is among the faculty members who have vocally opposed universities integrating AI into curricula with little to no input from faculty or their unions.

“This is the transformation of the very mission of public education, and it can’t just be implemented one way from the top,” said Ovetz, who is co-author of the forthcoming book “Organizing Against AI in Higher Education.”

SJSU formally launched an initiative called AI Everywhere in fall 2025 with the aim of ethically and responsibly integrating the technology throughout campus. The initiative is part of an effort to prepare students for the future workforce, said Sandra Hirsh, the university's special assistant to the provost for AI initiatives.

It’s also part of a larger push by the California State University system, which signed contracts with several AI-focused tech companies in 2025, including a nearly $17 million deal with Open AI to provide ChatGPT Edu to more than 460,000 students and 63,000 faculty and staff throughout its 22 campuses. CSU renewed its contract with OpenAI this year to the tune of $13 million annually for three years.

By the start of the 2027-28 academic year, SJSU expects to have 28 AI-related programs and 101 courses, Hirsh said. That would represent a 75% and 36% increase, respectively, since fall 2025.

The university also launched a host of new AI events and initiatives, including faculty training on one of tech company Nvidia’s platforms. In the same vein, it worked with the city of San Jose to open the AI Center for Civic and Social Good, a space at a joint public and university academic library where community residents, students and workers can use AI education tools, Hirsh said.

"This is the transformation of the very mission of public education, and it can’t just be implemented one way from the top."

While the scale of SJSU's AI efforts are significant, it's hardly the only institution to embrace the technology. Almost every institution is using or plans to use AI in some capacity, said Hironao Okahana, formerly managing researcher and chief of planning and impact at the American Council on Education.

In many places, that has translated into new courses. Over one-third of surveyed faculty members, 37%, said their institution had created new AI-focused classes, according to a report released in January by the American Association of Colleges and Universities and Elon University, which polled over 1,000 instructors in fall 2025.

And as of last July, a quarter of senior higher ed leaders reported adopting generative AI technologies at their institutions for student support, with another 22% planning to do so within the next six months, according to an ACE fall 2025 survey. Another 43% said they planned to adopt AI technologies within the next one to two years.

Many students, meanwhile, have issues with the ways large language models use data without their knowledge and consent, and how these systems reproduce and amplify existing forms of discrimination in society, among a host of other concerns, said Lindsay Weinberg, a professor and director of Purdue University’s Tech Justice Lab.

And some students are apprehensive about their universities forging partnerships with companies that use AI for military purposes, Weinberg said.

In April, SJSU students protested their university’s ties to companies including Nvidia, OpenAI, and Google for that reason, Spartan Media Group reported.

Many faculty share the students' concerns. And both faculty and students express misgivings about the environmental consequences from AI and how data centers cause noise pollution and negatively impact local water and electrical systems, said Weinberg.

Students are also hearing widespread reports about how AI will automate entry-level jobs, shrinking their job opportunities, Ovetz added.

Faculty have broad worries on AI’s impact on higher education, said C. Edward Watson, vice president for digital innovation at AAC&U and co-author of the association’s faculty survey report.

Nearly 52% of staff and faculty at CSU institutions indicated they were skeptical of the benefits of AI in higher education, with about 85% indicating they were concerned about the ethical use of the technology, according to 2025 system survey data.

A nationwide survey from AAC&U also highlighted additional sweeping concerns from faculty. About three-quarters of faculty said cheating spiked on their campuses when generative AI tools became widespread and that they believed the technology would impact degree integrity.

Meanwhile, nearly all faculty members, 95%, expressed concern about students becoming overreliant on AI tools, and large majorities indicated the technology would diminish students’ critical thinking skills and attention spans, according to the report.

Overall, Ovetz said, many educators say that by using AI, students are cheating themselves out of the reasons they are attending college — to learn and expand their understanding, consciousness and awareness.

For some students, their opposition to AI is part of broader resistance to technology and social media shifting from being optional to seemingly mandatory in today’s society — including on campuses, said Lucy Jackson, a sophomore at Binghamton University, in New York.

Jackson was an early member of the Luddite Club, a New York City-based anti-tech youth group that has turned into a global movement with roughly 40 campus and regional chapters over the past five years.

When students enroll at a university, they are expecting to receive an education, not a requirement that they have to interact with certain technologies, said Jackson.

But some students are using AI tools to help with their homework or exams, giving them an unfair advantage, and getting a “way out of education” — allowing them to earn a degree “without having to do much,” Jackson said.

“That creates a culture that isn’t necessarily something that everybody wants to live in or that everyone wants to follow,” said Jackson. “That’s where a lot of the pushback comes from.”

Despite the concerns, faculty aren’t broadly rejecting AI tools, Ovetz said. Many view generative AI as an interesting tool to incorporate into their teaching, for example by using chatbots to help students engage with course material, said Ovetz.

At CSU, 61% of faculty and staff said they regularly use AI-powered tools or applications when teaching, researching or performing their work, and 64% say it positively affects those tasks, according to the survey of CSU campuses.

Higher ed leaders have been grappling with how to integrate AI literacy into the curriculum, who should be teaching courses on how to use AI, what type of professional development should be required for instructors and what type of tools they should provide to faculty and students, Watson said.

While colleges and universities are good at adding new courses to meet student needs,they are not good at doing it fast, said Watson. It usually takes a few semesters of discussions with faculty and employers to determine what students need to know, he said.

That was reflected in the AAC&U survey, with 68% of faculty reporting their institutions had not prepared educators to use generative AI “for effective teaching and mentoring.” Less than half reported their institutions had written guidelines for AI use, and just 13% had adopted AI literacy as “a general learning outcome.”

That’s despite a widespread belief among faculty members that it’s important to teach students about issues related to AI during class, the AAC&U survey found. Large majorities stated it was important to teach students about inaccurate statements produced by GenAI tools, how the tools are used to produce misinformation and deep fakes, and how they erode copyright and authorship, according to the survey.

It has been largely left to individual professors to design or restructure their courses with AI, said Lee. The vast majority of faculty, 87%, said they have created their own policies on students’ use of generative AI for assessments, projects and tests, the survey found.

That’s not surprising, however, as decisions about what faculty teach are not usually made top-down, Lee said.

“The law and philosophy of academic freedom and trusting the learning expertise of the faculty leaves a little bit more of a hands-off orientation,” Lee said.

When administrators form partnerships with tech companies and push technologies without discussing those initiatives with unions or faculty members on their campuses, they often draw the ire of the students and employees most impacted by those decisions, said Weinberg.

Last year, the California Faculty Association filed an unfair labor practice charge against CSU, alleging the university system failed to notify the union when it launched an AI initiative in 2025 and refused to meet to discuss faculty rights and the impacts that it would have. CFA represents some 29,000 professors, lecturers and other workers at CSU.

The union complaint underscored the need for administrators to slow down on AI adoption so those initiatives can go through the shared governance process and be bargained over with academic unions, said Ovetz, who has been involved in the bargaining efforts.

CFA in February introduced an article in their negotiations with the university to protect faculty from being required to use the tools and provide them with intellectual property protections. Those negotiations have been ongoing for a year and a half, said Ovetz, but no agreement or contract has yet been reached.

“We are planning to vote to strike in the coming months, including over AI,” Ovetz said.

Those protections are important, as workloads increase for faculty who have to learn how to use AI tools as well as constantly check students' work to see if it was written using the technology, said Ovetz.

Many institutions that have succeeded in integrating AI for teaching or administrative purposes have established universitywide committees that discuss new pieces of technology and goals for using them, said Okahana. Those committees should include various campus stakeholder and constituent groups to help get people on board with new technologies, he said.

SJSU, for example, formed a 15-member AI advisory committee in 2024 composed of various university stakeholders, including faculty, students and staff, to advise administrators on how to ethically integrate AI tools throughout campus.

More than half of faculty reported their institutions had implemented a task force or group to oversee and manage implementation of AI tools across their campus, according to the AAC&U survey.

“Stakeholder engagement is really important and not just a tool to plug in,” said Okahana. “It is challenging us to think about how we do the teaching, how we run the university.”

Ovetz, however, said an advisory committee “is just that: advisory.”

CFA filed an unfair labor practice charge with the state, he said, “exactly because this violated our collective bargaining agreement and was implemented without bargaining with the faculty union.”

#7156. University of Maine offers buyouts to 158 faculty members as it cuts budget

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Leaders of the public institution say they are trying to minimize layoffs as they slash away at a $19 million structural deficit.

The University of Maine is offering voluntary buyouts to 158 faculty members as it works to slash its budget, senior leaders said Thursday.

Earlier this week, the public institution notified the eligible faculty members, who work at both its flagship campus in Orono as well as its Machias regional campus. Those eligible are over 55 and have worked at UMaine full time or its equivalent for 10 years.

Faculty who opt in will receive a one-time payment equal to their base annual salary once they retire on Sept. 1 of next year.

The university leaders noted that faculty unions at both campuses voted to move the buyout proposal forward. Both union and nonunion faculty are eligible for the early retirement packages.

“We are exploring every responsible option to close the projected $19 million structural gap while minimizing impacts to our dedicated faculty and staff and maintaining the affordable, high-quality education they provide our students to the fullest extent possible,” UMaine leaders said in a public message.

Just how many faculty agree to the buyout could affect layoff numbers, as university officials have already signaled job cuts are likely ahead as part of budget-cutting efforts.

So far, leadership has released loosely outlined plans to reduce UMaine’s academic budget by $10 million and its nonacademic budget by $6 million.

#7154. DOL final rule revamps antidiscrimination requirements for apprenticeships

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The U.S. Department of Labor issued a final rule to rescind “affirmative action requirements” for federally registered apprenticeships, including in education.

Education is the top occupation in the U.S. for degree-based apprenticeships, according to an August report by New America. Beyond teachers, K-12-related registered apprenticeship programs also include principals and lead teachers.

Registered Apprenticeship programs are meant to bridge the gap between employer needs and entry-level professionals, per the official federal apprenticeship fact sheet. What sets them apart is that they are industry-vetted and paid opportunities, they offer structured on-the-job learning and supplemental classroom education, and give participants portable credentials.

The programs span several industries, including agriculture, construction, education, energy, financial services, health care, hospitality, manufacturing, technology and transportation.

In the final rule, DOL summarized public commenters’ concerns with the new regulation’s potential effect on educator apprenticeship programs.

Specifically, one commenter said that students need access to teachers who they can relate to, who can “affirm their identities,” and who can “teach with an understanding of the cultural context of their lives,” according to DOL. Otherwise, the commenter said, students “may feel invisible,” and their learning outcomes may suffer when the teaching workforce is "homogeneous."

DOL noted that another commenter said the rule would “restrict active and expansive recruitment efforts that address gaps in the educator workforce.”

In response to those comments, DOL said the final rule reduces “prescriptive Federal mandates” that will provide apprenticeship sponsors — including in the education sector — greater flexibility to design their programs, such as with outreach and recruitment strategies.

“The Department acknowledges the important role that educator apprenticeship programs can play in fortifying the teaching workforce,” DOL’s response said. “The Department’s final rule does not prohibit sponsors from engaging in lawful recruitment efforts.”

More broadly, DOL “expects that removing these obligations will significantly reduce administrative burdens for sponsors,” and encourage further adoption of Registered Apprenticeship programs, the final rule document said.

Since 2024, DOL has continued to deregulate apprenticeship programs. DOL originally proposed a rule in December 2023 seeking to “modernize” apprenticeship programs. At the time, then-Acting Secretary of Labor Julie Su said in a press release, “Equity and job quality have marked the most successful Registered Apprenticeship programs for workers and employers alike. This proposed rule codifies the Department of Labor’s strong commitment to these principles.”

#7145. DOJ sues Penn State over in-state tuition for undocumented students

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The federal agency appears to be turning its sights on individual colleges after filing lawsuits over all such policies at the state level.

A Penn State spokesperson on Friday said the university is reviewing the DOJ’s complaint.

Undocumented students at Penn State are eligible to pay in-state tuition if they attended a Pennsylvania high school for at least four years and graduated with either a high school degree or a GED in the state, among other requirements.

In 2026-27, Penn State’s sticker price for in-state tuition at its main campus is $20,878. That’s less than half of what it charges out-of-state students, $44,574.

As in its state lawsuits, the DOJ argued in court documents that Penn State’s policy illegally provides benefits to undocumented students that are not available to all U.S. citizens.

Pennsylvania has not faced such a DOJ lawsuit as it does not offer in-state tuition to undocumented students at the state level.

Under federal immigration law, undocumented students cannot receive postsecondary education benefits based on state residency “unless a citizen or national of the United States” is also eligible regardless of residency. Some states fighting back against the DOJ in court have argued that their tuition residency policies apply equally to both citizens and U.S. residents regardless of legal status.

The federal agency has sued the following 24 states and Washington, D.C.

#7129. Gartner: Worldwide AI Spending to Reach $2.67 Trillion in 2026

The artificial intelligence industry is expected to generate $2.67 trillion in worldwide spending this year, according to a new forecast from Gartner. But relatively little of that money will be spent on the generative AI models dominating the industry's headlines.

The much bigger business is everything required to make them run.

Worldwide spending on AI infrastructure will reach nearly $1.5 trillion in 2026, accounting for about 56% of all AI spending. Gartner expects spending on generative AI models themselves to total just $28.3 billion. Put another way, for every dollar Gartner expects to be spent on generative AI models this year, more than $52 will be spent on AI infrastructure.

It is a striking measure of how the economics of the AI boom are changing. ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and other models may be the most visible products of the generative AI era, but much of the money is going to the enormous buildout of servers, semiconductors, networks, cloud capacity, and other infrastructure required to support them.

"The buildout of AI data center capacity is the largest infrastructure project humanity has ever undertaken," John-David Lovelock, distinguished vice president analyst at Gartner, said in announcing the forecast.

Gartner now expects worldwide AI spending to increase 49.5% this year, from about $1.79 trillion in 2025 to $2.67 trillion in 2026. Infrastructure represents $1.484 trillion of that total. The next-largest categories are AI services, at $576.5 billion, and AI software, at $461.6 billion. Spending on AI agents and assistants is expected to reach $29.2 billion, only slightly more than the $28.3 billion Gartner forecasts for generative AI models.

The numbers also show just how quickly expectations are changing. In January, Gartner forecast $2.53 trillion in total AI spending for 2026, including $1.37 trillion for infrastructure. By May, those forecasts had risen to $2.60 trillion and $1.43 trillion, respectively. The latest forecast raises them again, to $2.67 trillion overall and $1.48 trillion for infrastructure.

Since January, Gartner has added roughly $143 billion to its 2026 AI spending estimate. About $118 billion of that increase, or roughly 83%, comes from a higher infrastructure forecast.

That is significant because infrastructure was already, by far, the largest component of Gartner's AI spending estimate. As the firm's expectations for the AI market have risen during 2026, most of the additional money has accumulated there.

The infrastructure category is broad. Gartner includes AI-optimized cloud infrastructure, servers, networking, AI processors, and devices. Demand for those systems remains strong despite rising memory prices, Lovelock said.

"The capacity growth from hyperscalers and service providers purchasing AI-optimized servers will continue to be the largest single area of spending," he said.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Carahsoft has created a new community for K-12 schools and districts designed to help education leaders navigate AI adoption and cyber readiness.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

#7126. Avantis Education Adds New VR Math and Language Learning Experiences

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

EscapeRooms features five themed VR experiences in which students solve puzzles and answer rapid-fire questions to race against the clock and break free of a themed escape room. Designed for use with ClassVR headsets, the suite offers two-, four-, and eight-minute challenges — with themes such as "Shark Lab," "The Floor Is Lava," and "cursed Egyptian Tomb" — that help students build core skills and master complex mathematical concepts. The challenges are available in eight languages and can be adapted for different age ranges. Each Escape Room also includes a teacher's guide with starter activities and professional development materials.

LingoLab provides more than 60 learning experiences in immersive formats such as 360-degree videos and 3D models, designed to support Spanish and English language learning from beginner to upper-intermediate levels. Lessons are aligned with ACTFL proficiency guidelines and WIDA English Language Development standards, as well as CEFR and the Oxford 3000.

"With Eduverse+, our focus is on giving teachers immersive content that has a clear role within everyday teaching and learning. Listening to educators is a vital part of that process, and both EscapeRooms and LingoLab were shaped by feedback from schools and real classroom needs," said Manjinder Kaur, vice president of product for Avantis Education, in a statement. "While they are very different experiences, both are designed to give students meaningful opportunities to develop skills, receive feedback, and build confidence. By continuing to expand Eduverse+, we're giving schools more ways to use virtual reality technology across the curriculum and making immersive learning relevant to an even wider range of classroom needs."

EscapeRooms and LingoLab join the existing Eduverse+ library of content suites, including EduverseAI, WildWorld, STEAM3D, and CareerHub. The new libraries are available to EduVerse+ customers at no additional cost.

For more information, visit the ClassVR site here.

#7125. Casey McQuiston Predicted Texas’s Blue Wave Seven Years Ago in ‘Red, White & Royal Blue’ and Now They’re Manifesting a Talarico Collab (Exclusive)

Yes, they’ve seen the memes. And they’re absolutely living for all of it.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

The day after the midterm elections, Casey McQuiston, author of Red, White & Royal Blue, is wearing a black bleach tie-dyed shirt with “Texas” on it and joking about “being the main character on the internet—in a good way.”

In their New York Times bestseller, Alex Claremont-Diaz, son of the president of the United States—and boyfriend of Britain’s Prince Henry—orchestrates his Democratic mother’s victory in Texas. It’s a feat the president’s aides mocked him for even proposing and one that gives his mom the electoral college votes she needs to win a second term.

On Tuesday night, as election results showed Democratic candidates James Talarico (for the U.S. Senate from Texas) and Gina Hinojosa (a Texas gubernatorial hopeful) pulling out wins, fans of the book and its movie adaptation began posting memes and references celebrating “Blexas”—Blue Texas, duh—in response.

“Casey McQuiston, this is a WILD marketing budget for the new book & movie but I’m not mad about it,” said one post, a nod to the Red, White and Royal Blue sequel novella, Red, White & Royal Blue: The Private Correspondence, coming out on December 1.

“James Talarico clearly got ahold of Alex Claremont Diaz’s Texas Binder,” said another, referencing the giant file Alex kept his strategy in.

By 11 p.m., Casey had gotten in on the fun, reposting one fan’s meme with the text “keep sending me these i’m bout to bust,” to their Instagram Stories. Two of their favorite mentions came from playwright Jeremy O. Harris and their favorite skincare influencer.

Cosmopolitan talked with Casey less than 24 hours after the Democrats swept Texas about Election Night, predicting a Blue Texas, and what’s next for Alex and Henry.

I was at my sewing machine making some patchwork stuff for Christmas gifts. I had the Steve Kornacki live feed on my computer. I was just passively taking it in, trying not to get too emotionally involved because of what happened in 2024. That was really bad.

At some point, when it was getting close to calling the race for Talarico, I thought, Let’s move this to the living room and lock in, see what’s about to happen. Then I was under my weighted blanket, living for Kornacki. I’ve never seen him so expressive. I didn’t know he was allowed to make faces.

I’m not going to lie, I was walking on sunshine this morning, baby. I couldn’t be happier for Texas and the people I love who live there.

The one thing I’m not is surprised. I knew Texas had this in them all along. It was simply just a matter of having the resources and having the right stars aligning. I’m just so proud of them, and I’m so happy for them.

I’ve been sent so many memes. I generally have a lot of digital boundaries. I don’t look my name up. I stay out of reader spaces, but there are so many memes. I can’t resist.

I don’t think I can even compute that. It’s hard to even conceptualize myself as being part of any canon because when I think of that book, the image that comes to my mind is sitting in my bed at my mom’s house, typing it in Google Docs. It’s still that to me.

I’ve had all these different relationships with it over the 10 years that it’s been in my life. There have been times I found it hard to access the feelings of hope that went into it. There have been times I’ve found it difficult not to want to leave that feeling in the past because of what we’ve been through the last 10 years. It’s so hard to have optimism, and that book is optimistic. What keeps me coming back to it is that I had no idea whether things would get worse or better, but I believed they would get better.

Ultimately, I wrote what I saw: Texas’s heart was a lot more complicated than many people thought, and the people in the state deserve so much better. That’s what I keep coming back to when I think of that back.

First of all, let’s not forget how many fucking electoral votes Texas has. And if you wanted the big moment at the end where you have one state come in and save the election for you, it has to be Texas. It felt like it could be a huge twist, but I could also see it actually happening.

I wanted to do a story where my Democratic president was not a Kennedy but more of a [former Texas State Senator] Wendy Davis or Jimmy Carter. To me, Texas is where you go for that. A few years before, I had watched a livestream of Wendy Davis’s filibuster for abortion rights in Texas. I was so galvanized by that and so amazed to see there was a woman like that who held office in Texas. She really inspired me too, so it just felt right.

Also, in my experience, Texas is the most American of the states. I wanted the juxtaposition of the British aristocracy and the American gunslinger vibe.

No, I haven’t. But I’ve heard there’s a Red White fan on the Talarico campaign or someone who worked closely with them. I don’t blame anyone on any campaign for not necessarily wanting to campaign with a gay smut author before Election Day in a tight race. I’m like, Let’s play it safe. I’ll catch you after your victory lap.

I am hoping I’ll hear from the Talarico campaign. I would love to do a collab.

It’s such an important thing to bring up because it’s sneaky. The trans sports bans are such a weird vehicle conservatives are using right now. It’s a backdoor way of legislating people out of existence.

A lot of people look the other way because they might not have a trans person in their life directly affected by it or they might think there are so few trans athletes that this isn’t a real problem.

I think what we’ve learned from history is that it’s never that simple. It’s almost always boiling the frog in water, slowly raising the temperature to see what they can get away with. We really do have to keep our eye on the ball when it comes to anti-trans legislation, even if it seems frivolous.

1,000 percent. Romance from its inception is deeply political. So much of the early proto-romance was rooted in, Let’s write a how-to guide on how good women should behave so their virtue could be rewarded with true love. Then we had waves of the Austen of it all.

Going back to more recent years, the ’70s or so, so much of it was about centering agency and pleasure and, in some weird ways, even educating about sexuality and what your body can do other than be legislated, abused, and prevailed upon.

Anytime we have a juggernaut sub-industry of media that is predominantly driven by women and marginalized people, and those stories are centered on those people, that is hugely political. So, yes, I don’t think we can ever separate politics from romance. It’s baked into the pie.

Most of Cat Sebastian’s backlist I think of as really political queer romance. They’re not necessarily about politics, but politics are part of the journey we’re on.

When I think of political romance, I also think a lot about Beverly Jenkins. I think we all do. Much of her backlist, especially the stuff she was writing in the ’90s, is super political and fun to read.

They would be in Texas. Alex is hosting the watch party. He’s trying to keep it professional. Until 10 p.m., it was more of passed-apps vibe; after 10 p.m., it became a full-on, slam-down, hoedown fucking party. There was a lot of dancing and some light crowd surfing.

It would be one of those nights where you’re having the most fun celebrating something, and you’re looking at your partner across the room, and you’re locking eyes, and you’re both thinking, It’s on later when we’re alone. Then as soon as you’re alone, you just pass out.

Red, White & Royal Blue: The Private Correspondence is an epistolary novella. The concept is that after their emails were leaked in Red, White & Royal Blue, Alex and Henry swore they would never write emails to each other again. But they still want to write. So this is a collection of all of their handwritten letters, notes, cards, everything else over 20 years after the end of the first book. It covers everything from getting married to moving to Texas to starting a family and Texas electoral politics.

If you have been curious to know what happens to Alex and Henry after the end of the book, I got you. I’ve got 20 years’ worth of answers for you in this novella. It’s really fun. It’s silly, and it’s definitely horny at points and angsty too. But more than anything else, it takes a lot of what I loved from the first book: those emails where they’re really pouring their hearts out to each other, connecting so intimately through words, and letting that cook for you for, you know, 300 pages.

Elizabeth Held is a writer and avid reader in Washington, D.C. She writes What to Read If, a popular book recommendation newsletter, and is working on Romancelandia, a book about romance novels and people who love them, out in 2028.

#7121. Anne Hathaway and J.Lo Both Love Chocolate Cherry Nails: Here’s How to Wear Them This Fall

The chocolate-brown-meets-cherry-red combo is entirely irresistible.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

At this point, I know all too well that my fall manicure rotation will always include some variation of burgundy nails, chocolate brown nails, and black cherry nails. But what happens when you combine all three? You get chocolate cherry nails, the rich, red-meets-brown manicure that’s been getting a lot of love from Hollywood. Jennifer Lopez has given the moody shade her stamp of approval and Anne Hathaway stepped out wearing a similar cherry cocoa manicure earlier this year.

“Chocolate cherry is one of those wonderfully safe, sophisticated colors that simply goes with everything,” says nail artist Max Estrada. “It’s versatile enough to complement almost any wardrobe, which is precisely why I think celebrities gravitate towards it. It’s glamorous without demanding too much attention, but it certainly knows how to make an entrance.” From short, glossy nails à la Anne Hathaway to longer, dramatic tips inspired by J.Lo, chocolate cherry is proof that sometimes the coolest manicure trends are the simplest ones. Ahead, everything you need to know about getting the look, plus inspiration for your next nail appointment.

Chocolate cherry is the marriage of two fall nail colors: deep cherry red and chocolate brown. Unlike a traditional burgundy, which is more of a winey purple, chocolate cherry is a warmer with a brown undertone. (And it’s pretty dang expensive-looking, I might add.) “It’s incredibly easy to wear without being boring,” says Estrada. “You get the classic elegance of a red manicure mixed with the richness of chocolate brown, creating something that feels expensive, polished and a little mysterious.”

The color is also a perfect transitional shade for fall and winter manicures. As the weather cools off, “we naturally move away from the bright, playful colors of summer towards deeper, warmer tones,” says Estrada. “Chocolate cherry captures that transition perfectly. It has the romance of a deep red and the warmth of autumnal brown, making it an incredibly flattering seasonal color.”

The color looks gorgeous as a simple, solid-color manicure, but there’s also plenty of room to play around with different nail shapes, finishes, and designs. “Personally, I adore a beautifully tapered, long almond nail in chocolate cherry,” says Estrada. “There’s something incredibly vampy, sensual, and sophisticated about that silhouette, especially with an ultra-glossy finish.” He also likes the color with a square nail too.

If you want to get into some nail art, you can go simple or get adventurous with it: “I would also love chocolate cherry with a magnetic velvet cat-eye effect, creating that gorgeous multidimensional movement in the color,” Estrada says. “Or imagine a deep chocolate base with an exaggerated cherry-red French tip.”

Nail prep is always key to a professional-looking mani, especially when you’re working with darker polish, which can be less forgiving. If you’re doing a DIY manicure and you don’t know where to start, here are some tips from Estrada. First, push back your cuticles. “I always recommend gently pushing back the cuticle area, carefully removing excess dead cuticle tissue, refining the nail shape and ensuring every nail looks consistent,” he says.

Next, before applying polish, make sure your nails are clean and dry. “The nail plate should be properly cleansed and prepared before application, while the surrounding skin should be kept beautifully conditioned,” he adds. “Proper preparation means better adhesion, longer wear and a much more luxurious-looking manicure.”

Next, an insider tip: “One of my favorite professional techniques is leaving the tiniest clean margin between the polish and the surrounding skin,” he says. This way your polish is less likely to bleed onto the edges of your finger, which can look messy.

After you’ve applied the polish, he recommends finishing with a cuticle oil for hydration.

#7120. Study: AI Is Reshaping Work While Skills Erosion Remains Top Concern

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM. Critical thinking, judgment and the ability to evaluate AI output sit at the center of that tension.

The IBM Institute for Business Value study, "Designing the Thinking Organization," is based on two surveys conducted with Oxford Economics from April through June 2026. IBM surveyed 1,500 CHROs and senior executives responsible for workforce strategy across 21 geographies and 23 industries, along with 8,800 full-time employees across 28 countries.

The chart below, created by ChatGPT from IBM's data, summarizes the executive-employee differences around critical thinking and AI oversight along with several of the report's other findings. The graphic also includes IBM's findings on skill erosion, CHRO participation in AI strategy, workflow definitions, AI adoption inside HR, and reinvestment of AI productivity gains.

The findings extend beyond HR. More than half of employees surveyed — 52% — said their assigned tasks changed during the past year as AI reshaped day-to-day responsibilities. Yet only 26% of organizations clearly define work across human-led, AI-assisted, and AI-executed activities. IBM characterizes the situation as work changing faster than organizations are redesigning it, with day-to-day adaptation often taking the place of deliberate decisions about how people and AI should work together.

IBM found broad agreement between executives and employees on the importance of critical thinking and problem framing. Fifty-seven percent of executives and 49% of employees placed those capabilities near the top of the skills needed for AI-enabled work. A much larger divide emerged around evaluating AI itself: Seventy-one percent of executives prioritize the ability to supervise, validate, or override AI outputs, compared with 38% of employees — a 33-percentage-point gap.

An IBM announcement highlights another measure of that disconnect: Only 29% of employees rank judgment as important. The 29% figure measures employee prioritization of judgment and is distinct from the study page's 38% figure for supervising, validating, or overriding AI output.

The concern is not limited to skills workers still need to acquire. IBM said skill erosion is a top concern for 46% of executives, while 60% of employees say it directly affects them. The IBM Newsroom release says 60% of employees worry about skills erosion, with critical thinking cited most often as declining. Among employees who worry about erosion, IBM said three out of four report that AI has already begun to erode at least some of their skills.

IBM distinguishes that problem from a conventional skills gap. Eighty percent of enterprises have a reskilling roadmap for their workforce to collaborate with technology, according to the study. IBM argues that such programs address capabilities employees need to acquire but do not necessarily address existing skills that diminish as AI performs more of the underlying work.

#7116. Report: AI's Productivity Gains in Science Tempered by Time Spent Validating Outputs

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT. The findings add a scientific-workflow dimension to a much broader debate over unreliable, misaligned, and increasingly autonomous AI systems — without establishing that the more dramatic forms of AI misbehavior are occurring in scientific research.

Rather than the current, raging debate about "rogue AI" systems deliberately escaping control, deceiving scientists, or sabotaging experiments, the researchers are documenting something more immediate: AI output that requires auditing and verification, concerns about growing quantities of low-quality research, and a research pipeline in which faster hypothesis generation can run ahead of human and physical capacity to validate the results. The paper cites worries that AI-generated content, hallucinations, and what prior researchers have called illusions of understanding could strain peer review and validation.

The September paper, AI in Science: Early Insights, combines three main empirical sources: approximately 15 million anonymized Gemini interactions, an inventory of 2,690 specialized scientific AI models, and a survey of 637 active scientists in the United States and United Kingdom. Nearly half of the surveyed scientists reported using some form of AI every day, and researchers who reported net time savings averaged just under seven hours a week.

Google separately highlighted the findings in its Sept. 15 AI & Economy ATLAS update. The company emphasized both the productivity gains and the counterweight: Scientists were spending significant time validating AI output, accumulating hypotheses that still needed testing, and encountering bottlenecks in physical experimentation and clinical validation. Google said those constraints mean faster individual tasks may not immediately translate into more discoveries.

Productivity findings are substantial. Around three-quarters of surveyed researchers reported saving time through AI, and the average reported net saving was almost seven hours per week. Researchers said much of that time went back into research. The paper also reports that about 68% perceived greater access to insights from other disciplines, while roughly 65% said AI had increased the breadth of their research agendas.

But the authors found that checking AI output consumes a meaningful share of those gains. Among scientists who reported saving time, about 89% said more than 10% of the saved time went into verifying, debugging, or fact-checking AI output. About 46% said more than one-quarter of their saved time went to that work. The authors describe this as a verification tax associated with the high value science places on reliable and correct results.

#7112. More Oversight: California Governor Proposes Independent Watchdogs Within AI Safety System

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

The effort could eventually include placing outside evaluators inside frontier AI laboratories and requiring companies to maintain an independently tested emergency shutdown mechanism, commonly described as a "kill switch."

Those requirements are not yet California law.

Newsom's Sept. 18 executive order directs state officials to study the proposals and recommend possible changes to existing law by Nov. 16. It does not immediately require OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, or other developers to install a shutdown mechanism.

The distinction matters because the phrase "AI kill switch" suggests a technical safeguard California has already imposed. For now, it is one element of a broader oversight system the state is assembling around the developers of its most powerful AI models.

Under the order, the California Government Operations Agency and the Governor's Office of Emergency Services must consult outside experts about whether the state should require large frontier developers to embed independent verification organizations in their laboratories.

The evaluators could conduct periodic audits and determine whether company safety frameworks and risk assessments meet adequate standards. They could also test whether an emergency shutdown mechanism would work as intended.

The order asks officials to consider expanding the definition of a reportable critical safety incident to include cases in which an AI system appears to evade controls or operate beyond its intended boundaries.

Newsom said the action was prompted by recent incidents involving AI agents defeating security restrictions, as well as the absence of comprehensive federal legislation.

"We're not waiting to act," Newsom said in the state's announcement of the order. "We're going to speed up our work on substantial and responsible AI oversight before it's too late."

The executive order builds on two laws Newsom signed on Sept. 9. Together, Senate Bill 813 and Assembly Bill 1405 begin regulating the organizations hired to inspect AI systems.

California's earlier frontier AI law, SB 53, requires large developers to publish safety frameworks and report specified critical incidents. The new laws begin addressing a separate question: Who checks whether those disclosures and assessments are reliable?

SB 813 requires the Government Operations Agency to develop standards for designating independent verification organizations, or IVOs. Those organizations must demonstrate that they have the expertise to assess AI risks and explain the measurements and methods supporting their conclusions.

#7111. Skyler Gisondo on His New Fashion Campaign—and Dressing Like Harrison Ford in the ’90s

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Primetime star is gearing up in Bonobos and getting ready for a very big 2027.

Skyler Gisondo is remarkably easy to talk to. If you’ve seen him on one of your various screens—whether as Decoy Dan from Primetime, Jimmy Olsen from Superman, or maybe just that meme of him from a decade-old red-carpet photo—you probably assumed as much. It’s the big smile, the friendly eyes, the freckles, the general sense of wholesomeness. But I can confirm it, because even though I know we’ve got a relatively brief window of time to discuss his new campaign with men’s fashion brand Bonobos over Zoom, we spend the first few minutes of our chat just … shooting the shit. It feels like catching up with a buddy you haven’t seen in a while. Relaxed. Natural.

Thankfully, the vibe continues as we start digging into matters of style. Gisondo tells me he’s always been interested in dressing well but hasn’t always known where to start. “I would see a cool dude walking down the street or something, and think, ‘Man, I want to look like that guy.’ But I never knew how. I always felt lost,” he says. He describes his 20s as a decade of “desperately trying on shoes that didn’t fit or something.”

He turned 30 recently, though, and “I really feel like over the last couple of years, I’ve homed in on my sense of style, what I feel good and confident in.” A very good time, then, to star in a fashion campaign—especially considering this seems like a breakout moment for the actor. He’s been working consistently since he was a kid, but his performance in Primetime as a young actor who gradually realizes just how toxic the entertainment industry can be has clearly launched him to another level. And he still has his upcoming role in Focker-in-Law this November and a couple more appearances as Jimmy Olsen (in Man of Tomorrow on the big screen and The People v. Gorilla Grodd on television) on the docket for 2027.

Over the course of our conversation, we touch on everything from dressing like a cowboy (a mistake) to using Harrison Ford in the ’90s as your style North Star (a very good idea). We also chat about the stuff he’s stolen from sets for his own wardrobe, the psychological validation of joining the Shrek franchise, and why, after Primetime, we can expect more creative big swings in the future. Read on for a few highlights.

This interview has been condensed and edited.

“It’s ultimately about your own confidence. I feel like the sweet spot for fashion is when what you look like reflects how you feel inside—when you get to this equilibrium and it allows you to be a more fully realized version of yourself. We all grow and our sense of fashion evolves with us but ultimately like I feel like there should be some current that runs through all of it. The stuff I’m drawn to is the more timeless, classic stuff. I look at Harrison Ford in the ’90s and I’m just like, it doesn’t get cooler, you know?”

“For a while, for whatever reason, me and every actor I know were like trying to look like a cowboy. And it just hit me one day: ‘None of us have ever worked a day in our lives on a cattle ranch, man.’ I don’t even know how to use a hammer! Like, what are we doing? Can we just be ourselves?”

“I’m pretty active. I surf and hike a lot when I’m at home. And I love walking; if I have an hour or two between meetings, I’ll just walk a few miles and take calls. I want something comfortable and functional, and I find a lot of vintage workwear just hits that and fits me well. And the story is so compelling to me. The idea of ‘Who’s worn this? What were they getting up to?’ ”

“What drew me to the campaign and to the clothes is that there’s a kind of timelessness to that style. My mom thought I looked amazing in the photos. My girlfriend too. My grandma and my sisters thought I looked great. It’s this very accessible and sort of classically cool thing they have going on. And I felt like me. I felt confident—so much so that I stole several items from the set.”

“With shows and movies, the fear is that it’s a one-of-one item that you might need back for another season. But that’s when you hit them with the ‘pretty, pretty please?’ You say, ‘I swear to God, I’ll guard it with my life.’ And they say, ‘Okay, but you can’t tell anybody.’ With a campaign, it’s probably easier because they’re excited for you to have it and represent. But probably 80 percent of my closet is basically just clothes stolen from characters and sets. It’s fun because you get to try on a bunch of versions of yourself and learn what you like.

“For instance, with Jimmy Olsen in the first Superman movie, they did a fantastic job with this retro-future thing. And then in The People v. Gorilla Grodd, they went a little more vintage-y in a way that felt more like me, Skyler, personally. I tried to steal almost the entire closet.”

“Well, right now I’m doing press for Primetime and Focker-in-Law. I’m doing another movie for A24 called Goblin that I’m not sure how much I can say about; I really don’t want to get fired. But I’m so excited. It’s the most insane script I think I have ever read, and it’s definitely a big swing. We have Man of Tomorrow coming out in the summer, and then Shrek 5. The number of texts I got when I got Shrek … ‘You made it, man! Oh my God! I had no idea you made it!’ I was like, ‘Thank you. Yeah, baby! Shrek 5!’

“But ultimately, it’s a very exciting moment because it’s the first time I have people asking, ‘What do you want to do?’ And I’m like, ‘Damn! I never really thought about that!’ I feel like Primetime lit a fire under me in a real way. And even though I’ve kind of been doing this my whole life, I feel a sense of drive that I’ve never felt really in this way before. So, yeah, man, I’m excited.”

#7108. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

#7107. Met Gala 2027: Costume Institute Announces New Theme After John Galliano Controversy

The 2027 Met Gala has a new focus. On November 5, Vogue, alongside the Metropolitan Museum of Art Costume Institute, announced its new spring 2027 exhibition and Met Gala theme. And its new focus is on the “extraORDINARY: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday,” which will be curated by Andrew Bolton alongside Stephanie Kramer and Emily Mushaben. This pivot comes after designer John Galliano pulled out of the 2027 Met Gala months after political and civic leaders raised concerns over his previous racist and antisemitic comments that led to his termination as Dior’s creative director in 2011.

The exhibition will be “devoted to the undersung beauty and allure of quotidian garments—think the aforementioned t-shirts and jeans, plus raincoats, sweatshirts, puffer jackets, and more. It will ask visitors ‘to see the everyday anew, and come at its subject from two perspectives: First, exploring how designers have reinvented and elevated archetypal garments to runway-worthy creations and then, secondly, highlight how humble, even ‘disposable’ materials—like paper, aluminum, and plastic—are transformed into wearable art.”

“It was really important to come up with an exhibition that wasn’t just a masterwork show or something that we could do a little bit more easily,” Bolton also said. “That was really important to me: to come up with an idea that wasn’t a substitute. And it had to have some sort of contemporary resonance.”

Before this switch, the museum stated that the original exhibit, John Galliano: Horizons, was meant to “consider how artistic achievement should be understood alongside ethical accountability.” So now, this exploration of the extraORDINARY seems to be more focused on design and creation from a more sustainable viewpoint.

So now that fashion’s biggest night is back on track with a new theme in hand, it will be interesting to see how all our favorite celebrities interpret this new theme. The Met Gala 2027 will take place on May 3, and the exhibit will open to the public on May 9, 2027, and run through January 9, 2028.

#7104. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#7100. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.
